|\upgamma_{IJB}+\upgamma_{JBI}|\lesssim t^{-(\widetilde{q}_{I}+\widetilde{q}_{J}-\widetilde{q}_{B})}
.
This makes the significance of the stability condition (
1.8
) for
equation (
5.19
) clear: under this condition,
the quantity
\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}I,J,B=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}\\
I<J\end{subarray}}{\max}|\upgamma_{IJB}+\upgamma_{JBI}|

is integrable in
t
near
0
,
and by (
5.22
) (cf. Remark
1.2
),

\underset{I,J,B=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}{\max}|\upgamma_{IJB}|
is also integrable in
t
.
In our ensuing analysis, we will in fact control the terms on RHS (
5.19
) and
show that
\underset{I,J,B=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}{\max}|\upgamma_{IJB}|
is integrable,
which is a crucial step in our proof of stable blowup.

Remark 5.9
.

Interestingly, if we were to try to control the
\upgamma_{IJB}
’s at the low derivative levels
by using the formula (
2.27
) and separately controlling each of the factors

e_{I}^{i},\upomega_{i}^{I}
,
then we would not be able to close our estimates for the full range of Kasner
exponents verifying the stability condition (
1.8
).
In fact, since RHS (
2.27
) is cubic in
e_{I}^{i},\upomega_{i}^{I}
and their derivatives,
the crudest version of that approach would yield only

|\upgamma_{IJB}|\lesssim t^{-3q}
, which, when
q
is near
1
, is far too singular
for proving stability. Moreover, the evolution equation (
2.22b
)
for the
\upgamma_{IJB}
’s is not diagonal at the linear level and thus, a crude treatment based only
on this equation would lead to far too singular estimates
38
38

38

However, the structure of equation (
2.22b
)
is sufficient for our top-order energy estimates, which are allowed to be much more singular
within the scope of our approach; this explains why in Lemma
5.16
,
we derive commuted versions of equation (
2.22b
) to set up our energy estimates for
\upgamma
and
k
.

for the connection coefficients at the lower derivative levels.
Thus, the diagonal structure revealed by Proposition
5.7

is essential to our overall argument.

Proof of Proposition
5.7
.

Equations
(
5.19
)
follow from
the evolution equation (
2.22b
),
the definition of the background Kasner scalar functions in (
2.58
),
the antisymmetry property (
2.20
),
and straightforward algebraic computations.
(
5.20
)
then follows from differentiating (
5.19
) with
\partial^{\iota}
,
applying the product rule,
multiplying both sides of the resulting identity by
t^{q}
,
and then commuting the factor of
t^{q}
under the operator
\partial_{t}
on the LHS
and accounting for the commutator
[t^{q},\partial_{t}]
.

(
5.22
) is an immediate consequence of the Koszul formula for an orthonormal frame and the antisymmetry property (
2.20
).
∎

5.5.2
Pointwise estimates for the error terms in the structure coefficient evolution equations

In the next lemma, we derive pointwise estimates at the low derivative levels
for the error terms from Proposition
5.7
.

Lemma 5.10

(Pointwise estimates for the error terms in the structure coefficient evolution equations at
orders
\leq N_{0}
)
.

Recall that
\mathbb{D}(t)
is the total norm of the dynamic variables from Definition
3.1
.
Assume that the bootstrap assumptions (
3.10
) hold.
There exists a constant
C=C_{N,N_{0},A_{*},\mathfrak{D},q,\upsigma}>0

such that if
N_{0}\geq 1
and
N
is sufficiently large in a manner
that depends on
N_{0},A_{*},\mathfrak{D},q,
and
\upsigma
,
and if
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small
(in a manner that depends on
N,N_{0},A_{*},\mathfrak{D},q,
and
\upsigma
),
then the following pointwise estimates hold on
(T_{\textnormal{Boot}},1]\times\mathbb{T}^{\mathfrak{D}}

for the error terms

\mathfrak{S}_{IJB}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}

and

\mathfrak{S}_{IJB}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}

defined in (
5.21a
)–(
5.21b
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I,J,B=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}\\
I<J\end{subarray}}t^{q}|\mathfrak{S}_{IJB}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}|(t,x)&\leq C\varepsilon t^{q-1}\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}}\sum_{I,J,B=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}|\partial^{\iota}\upgamma_{IJB}|(t,x)\\
&\ \ +C\varepsilon t^{q-1}\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}}\sum_{I,i=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}|\partial^{\iota}(e_{I}^{i}-\widetilde{e}_{I}^{i})|(t,x),\end{split}

(5.23a)

\displaystyle\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I,J,B=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}\\
I<J\end{subarray}}t^{q}|\mathfrak{S}_{IJB}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}|(t,x)

\displaystyle\leq Ct^{-1+\upsigma}\mathbb{D}(t).

(5.23b)

Proof.

Based on equations (
5.21a
)–(
5.21b
),
the estimates
(
5.23a
)–(
5.23b
)
follow as straightforward consequences of
(
2.58
),
the inequalities in (
3.4
),
Definition
3.1
,
the bootstrap assumptions (
3.10
),
and the already derived low order estimates (
5.4
) for
n
.
Note in particular that we have used the fact that
the low order norm (
3.9a
) controls
k-\widetilde{k}

at derivative levels
\leq N_{0}+1
(see Remark
3.2
);
for example, for
|\iota|\leq N_{0}
,
this allows us to pointwise bound the magnitude of the sum

\sum_{\iota_{1}\cup\iota_{2}=\iota}n\cdot\partial^{\iota_{1}}(e-\widetilde{e})\cdot\partial\partial^{\iota_{2}}k
on RHS (
5.21a
)
by
\lesssim\varepsilon t^{-1}\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}}|\partial^{\iota}(e-\widetilde{e})|(t,x)
.
∎

5.5.3
Absence of certain structure coefficients in polarized
U(1)
-symmetry

In the next lemma, we show that for polarized
U(1)
-symmetric metrics with
\mathfrak{D}=3
,
relative to an orthonormal spatial frame of the type provided by Lemma
2.4
,
all structure coefficients with three distinct indices vanish.
As we explained in Remark
1.3
,
this vanishing is crucial for
the proof of our main results in the case of the
Einstein-vacuum equations in
1+3
dimensions
under polarized
U(1)
-symmetry.

Lemma 5.11

(The vanishing of key variables in polarized
U(1)
-symmetry)
.

Suppose that
\mathfrak{D}=3
and that

{\bf g}
is a polarized
U(1)
-symmetric metric
satisfying the hypotheses and conclusions of Lemma
2.4
.
Moreover, let
\{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\}
be an orthonormal spatial frame satisfying
the hypotheses and conclusions of Lemma
2.4
.
In particular,
e_{3}=(g_{33})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{3}

and
\mathcal{L}_{\partial_{3}}e_{I}=0
for
I=1,2,3
,
where
\partial_{3}
is the hypersurface-orthogonal Killing vectorfield. Then the following spatial connection coefficients vanish:

\displaystyle\upgamma_{123}=\upgamma_{231}=\upgamma_{312}=0.

(5.24)

Moreover, under the same assumptions, we have:

\displaystyle\upgamma_{IJB}+\upgamma_{JBI}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}0,&\mbox{ if }I=J,\\
0,&\mbox{ if }I,J,B\mbox{ are distinct}.\end{cases}

(5.25)

Proof.

Under the assumptions and conclusions of Lemma
2.4
,

\partial_{3}
is parallel to
e_{3}
and orthogonal to
\partial_{1}
and
\partial_{2}
,
and we have
e_{1}^{3}=e_{2}^{3}=e_{3}^{1}=e_{3}^{2}=\upomega_{3}^{1}=\upomega_{3}^{2}=\upomega_{1}^{3}=\upomega_{2}^{3}=e_{3}e_{I}^{i}=0
.
Hence, using (
2.27
) we compute:

\displaystyle\upgamma_{123}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2}\left\{\upomega_{l}^{3}(e_{1}e_{2}^{l}-e_{2}e_{1}^{l})-\upomega_{l}^{1}(e_{2}e_{3}^{l}-e_{3}e_{2}^{l})+\upomega_{l}^{2}(e_{3}e_{1}^{l}-e_{1}e_{3}^{l})\right\}=0,

\displaystyle\upgamma_{231}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2}\left\{\upomega_{l}^{1}(e_{2}e_{3}^{l}-e_{3}e_{2}^{l})-\upomega_{l}^{2}(e_{3}e_{1}^{l}-e_{1}e_{3}^{l})+\upomega_{l}^{3}(e_{1}e_{2}^{l}-e_{2}e_{1}^{l})\right\}=0,

\displaystyle\upgamma_{312}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2}\left\{\upomega_{l}^{2}(e_{3}e_{1}^{l}-e_{1}e_{3}^{l})-\upomega_{l}^{3}(e_{1}e_{2}^{l}-e_{2}e_{1}^{l})+\upomega_{l}^{1}(e_{2}e_{3}^{l}-e_{3}e_{2}^{l})\right\}=0,

which yields (
5.24
). A more conceptual justification of the above computations is that in the present setting,

g([e_{I},e_{J}],e_{B})=0

whenever
I,J,B
are distinct indices;
using this fact and the Koszul formula, we conclude that
\upgamma_{IJB}=0

whenever
I,J,B
are distinct indices, as desired.

(
5.25
) follows from (
5.24
)
and the antisymmetry property (
2.20
).
∎

Remark 5.12

(The role of polarized
U(1)
-symmetry)
.

In proving our stable Big Bang formation results for the Einstein-vacuum equations in
1+3
dimensions,
there is precisely one way in which our polarized
U(1)
-symmetry assumption is important for our analysis:
it allows us to use the results of Lemma
5.11
;
see also Remark
1.3

and the end of the proof of Proposition
5.26
.
Put differently, if we were to assume the conclusions
(
5.24
)–(
5.25
) of the lemma, then
the rest of our proof of stable Big Bang formation would go through.

5.5.4
Commuted evolution equations for
e
and
\upomega

In this section, we provide the evolution equations that we will use to control
the scalar functions

\{e_{I}^{i}\}_{I,i=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}

and

\{\upomega_{i}^{I}\}_{I,i=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}

as well as their derivatives.

Lemma 5.13

(Evolution equations for

\{e_{I}^{i}\}_{I,i=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}
,

\{\upomega_{i}^{I}\}_{I,i=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}
,
and their derivatives)
.

The evolution equations (
2.23a
)–(
2.23b
)
can be rewritten as follows,
where the Kasner background scalars

\{\widetilde{e}_{I}^{i}\}_{I,i=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}
,

\{\widetilde{\upomega}_{i}^{I}\}_{I,i=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}
,
and

\{\widetilde{k}_{IJ}\}_{I,J=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}

are defined in (
2.58
)
(see also Remark
2.6
),
and we recall that we do not sum over repeated underlined indices:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{t}({e_{I}^{i}}-\widetilde{e}_{I}^{i})+\frac{\widetilde{q}_{\underline{I}}}{t}({e_{\underline{I}}^{i}}-\widetilde{e}_{\underline{I}}^{i})&=(n-1)k_{IC}(e_{C}^{i}-\widetilde{e}_{C}^{i})+(k_{IC}-\widetilde{k}_{IC})(e_{C}^{i}-\widetilde{e}_{C}^{i})\\
&\ \ +(n-1)\widetilde{k}_{IC}\widetilde{e}_{C}^{i}+n(k_{IC}-\widetilde{k}_{IC})\widetilde{e}_{C}^{i},\end{split}

(5.26a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{t}(\upomega_{i}^{I}-\widetilde{\upomega}_{i}^{I})-\frac{\widetilde{q}_{\underline{I}}}{t}(\upomega_{i}^{\underline{I}}-\widetilde{\upomega}_{i}^{\underline{I}})&=-(n-1)k_{IC}(\upomega_{i}^{C}-\widetilde{\upomega}_{i}^{C})-(k_{IC}-\widetilde{k}_{IC})(\upomega_{i}^{C}-\widetilde{\upomega}_{i}^{C})\\
&\ \ -(n-1)\widetilde{k}_{IC}\widetilde{\upomega}_{i}^{C}-n(k_{IC}-\widetilde{k}_{IC})\widetilde{\upomega}_{i}^{C}.\end{split}

(5.26b)

Moreover, let
\iota
be a spatial multi-index with
|\iota|\leq N
,
and let
P\geq 0
be a real number.
Then the following equations hold:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}[t^{P}\partial^{\iota}({e_{I}^{i}}-\widetilde{e}_{I}^{i})]

\displaystyle=(P-\widetilde{q}_{\underline{I}})t^{P-1}\partial^{\iota}({e_{\underline{I}}^{i}}-\widetilde{e}_{\underline{I}}^{i})+t^{P}\mathfrak{E}_{I}^{i;(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}+t^{P}\mathfrak{E}_{I}^{i;(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)},

(5.27a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}[t^{P}\partial^{\iota}(\upomega^{I}_{i}-\widetilde{\upomega}^{I}_{i})]

\displaystyle=(P+\widetilde{q}_{\underline{I}})t^{P-1}\partial^{\iota}(\upomega^{I}_{i}-\widetilde{\upomega}^{I}_{i})+t^{P}\mathfrak{O}_{I}^{i;(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}+t^{P}\mathfrak{O}_{I}^{i;(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)},

(5.27b)

where:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{E}_{I}^{i;(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\iota_{1}\cup\iota_{2}\cup\iota_{3}=\iota}\partial^{\iota_{1}}(n-1)\cdot\partial^{\iota_{2}}k\cdot\partial^{\iota_{3}}(e-\widetilde{e})+\sum_{\iota_{1}\cup\iota_{2}=\iota}\partial^{\iota_{1}}(k-\widetilde{k})\cdot\partial^{\iota_{2}}(e-\widetilde{e}),

(5.28a)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{E}_{I}^{i;(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}

\displaystyle:=\partial^{\iota}(n-1)\cdot\widetilde{k}\cdot\widetilde{e}+\sum_{\iota_{1}\cup\iota_{2}=\iota}\partial^{\iota_{1}}n\cdot\partial^{\iota_{2}}(k-\widetilde{k})\cdot\widetilde{e},

(5.28b)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{O}_{I}^{i;(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\iota_{1}\cup\iota_{2}\cup\iota_{3}=\iota}\partial^{\iota_{1}}(n-1)\cdot\partial^{\iota_{2}}k\cdot\partial^{\iota_{3}}(\upomega-\widetilde{\upomega})+\sum_{\iota_{1}\cup\iota_{2}=\iota}\partial^{\iota_{1}}(k-\widetilde{k})\cdot\partial^{\iota_{2}}(\upomega-\widetilde{\upomega}),

(5.28c)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{O}_{I}^{i;(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}

\displaystyle:=\partial^{\iota}(n-1)\cdot\widetilde{k}\cdot\widetilde{\upomega}+\sum_{\iota_{1}\cup\iota_{2}=\iota}\partial^{\iota_{1}}n\cdot\partial^{\iota_{2}}(k-\widetilde{k})\cdot\widetilde{\upomega}.

(5.28d)

Proof.

(
5.26a
)–(
5.26b
)
follow from equations (
2.23a
)–(
2.23b
)
and straightforward algebraic computations.
(
5.27a
)–(
5.27b
)
then follow from differentiating (
5.26a
)–(
5.26b
)
with
\partial^{\iota}
,
using the Leibniz rule,
multiplying both sides of the resulting equations by
t^{P}
,
and commuting the factors of
t^{P}
under the operator
\partial_{t}
on the LHSs
and accounting for the commutator
[t^{P},\partial_{t}]
.
∎

\upsigma

\varepsilon

Proof.

.
39

5.5.7
Commuted equations for
k
and
\upgamma

In this section, we provide the evolution equations that we will use to control
the scalar functions

\{k_{IJ}\}_{I,J=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}

and

\{\upgamma_{IJB}\}_{I,J,B=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}

as well as their derivatives.

Lemma 5.16

(
\partial^{\iota}
-commuted equations for
\upgamma
and
k
)
.

Let
\iota
be a spatial multi-index with
|\iota|\leq N
,
and let
P\geq 0
be a real number.
Then for solutions to the equations of Proposition
2.2
,
the following evolution equations hold,
where the Kasner background scalars
\{\widetilde{k}_{IJ}\}_{I,J=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}

and
\widetilde{\psi}

are defined in (
2.58
)
(see also Remark
2.6
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{t}[t^{P}\partial^{\iota}(k_{IJ}-\widetilde{k}_{IJ})]&=(P-1)t^{P-1}\partial^{\iota}(k_{IJ}-\widetilde{k}_{IJ})+t^{P}ne_{C}\partial^{\iota}\upgamma_{IJC}-t^{P}ne_{I}\partial^{\iota}\upgamma_{CJC}-t^{P}e_{I}\partial^{\iota}e_{J}n\\
&\ \ +t^{P-1}\mathfrak{K}_{IJ}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}+t^{P}\mathfrak{K}_{IJ}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)},\end{split}

(5.31a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{t}(t^{P}\partial^{\iota}\upgamma_{IJB})&=Pt^{P-1}\partial^{\iota}\upgamma_{IJB}+t^{P}ne_{B}\partial^{\iota}k_{JI}-t^{P}ne_{J}\partial^{\iota}k_{BI}\\
&\ \ +t^{P}\mathfrak{G}_{IJB}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}+t^{P}\mathfrak{G}_{IJB}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)},\end{split}

(5.31b)

\displaystyle t^{P}e_{C}\partial^{\iota}k_{CI}

\displaystyle=t^{P}\mathfrak{M}_{I}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}+t^{P}\mathfrak{M}_{I}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)},

(5.31c)

where:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}_{IJ}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}

\displaystyle:=\partial^{\iota}(n-1)\cdot\widetilde{k}+\sum_{\iota_{1}\cup\iota_{2}=\iota}\partial^{\iota_{1}}(n-1)\cdot\partial^{\iota_{2}}(k-\widetilde{k}),

(5.32a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathfrak{K}_{IJ}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}&:=\sum_{\iota_{1}\cup\iota_{2}=\iota,\,|\iota_{2}|<|\iota|}\partial^{\iota_{1}}e\cdot\partial\partial^{\iota_{2}}\vec{e}n+\sum_{\iota_{1}\cup\iota_{2}=\iota,\,|\iota_{2}|<|\iota|}\partial^{\iota_{1}}\upgamma\cdot\partial^{\iota_{2}}\vec{e}n\\
&\ \ +\sum_{\iota_{1}\cup\iota_{2}\cup\iota_{3}=\iota,\,|\iota_{3}|<|\iota|}\partial^{\iota_{1}}n\cdot\partial^{\iota_{2}}e\cdot\partial^{\iota_{3}}\partial\upgamma+\sum_{v\in\{\upgamma,\vec{e}\psi\}}\sum_{\iota_{1}\cup\iota_{2}\cup\iota_{3}=\iota}\partial^{\iota_{1}}n\cdot\partial^{\iota_{2}}v\cdot\partial^{\iota_{3}}v,\end{split}

(5.32b)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{G}_{IJB}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}

\displaystyle:=n\cdot\widetilde{k}\cdot\partial^{\iota}\upgamma+\sum_{\iota_{1}\cup\iota_{2}=\iota}n\partial^{\iota_{1}}(k-\widetilde{k})\cdot\partial^{\iota_{2}}\upgamma+\widetilde{k}\cdot\partial^{\iota}\vec{e}n+\sum_{\iota_{1}\cup\iota_{2}=\iota}\partial^{\iota_{1}}(k-\widetilde{k})\cdot\partial^{\iota_{2}}\vec{e}n,

(5.32c)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{G}_{IJB}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\iota_{1}\cup\iota_{2}\cup\iota_{3}=\iota,\,|\iota_{1}|\geq 1}\partial^{\iota_{1}}n\cdot\partial^{\iota_{2}}k\cdot\partial^{\iota_{3}}\upgamma+\sum_{\iota_{1}\cup\iota_{2}\cup\iota_{3}=\iota,\,|\iota_{3}|<|\iota|}\partial^{\iota_{1}}n\cdot\partial^{\iota_{2}}e\cdot\partial^{\iota_{3}}\partial k,

(5.32d)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}_{I}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}

\displaystyle:=\widetilde{k}\cdot\partial^{\iota}\upgamma+\sum_{\iota_{1}\cup\iota_{2}=\iota}\partial^{\iota_{1}}(k-\widetilde{k})\cdot\partial^{\iota_{2}}\upgamma+\partial_{t}\widetilde{\psi}\cdot\partial^{\iota}\vec{e}\psi+\sum_{\iota_{1}\cup\iota_{2}=\iota}\partial^{\iota_{1}}(e_{0}\psi-\partial_{t}\widetilde{\psi})\cdot\partial^{\iota_{2}}\vec{e}\psi,

(5.32e)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}_{I}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\iota_{1}\cup\iota_{2}=\iota,\,|\iota_{2}|<|\iota|}\partial^{\iota_{1}}e\cdot\partial\partial^{\iota_{2}}k.

(5.32f)

Proof.

Equations (
5.31a
)–(
5.31b
)
follow from straightforward computations
based on first multiplying equations (
2.22a
)–(
2.22b
) by
n
,
using that
\partial_{t}=ne_{0}
,
differentiating the resulting equations with
\partial^{\iota}
,
applying the Leibniz rule,
multiplying both sides of the resulting identities by
t^{P}
,
and then commuting the factor of
t^{P}
under the operator
\partial_{t}
on the LHSs
and accounting for the commutator
[t^{P},\partial_{t}]
.
Similarly, equation (
5.31c
)
follows from
differentiating equation (
2.26b
) with
\partial^{\iota}
,
applying the Leibniz rule,
and then multiplying both sides of the resulting identity by
t^{P}
.
∎

5.5.8
Pointwise estimates for the error terms in the spatial metric evolution equations

Lemma 5.17

(Pointwise estimates for the error terms in the evolution equations for

\partial^{\leq N_{0}+1}(k-\widetilde{k})
)
.

Recall that
\mathbb{D}(t)
is the total norm of the dynamic variables from Definition
3.1
.
Assume that the bootstrap assumptions (
3.10
) hold.
There exists a constant
C=C_{N,N_{0},A_{*},\mathfrak{D},q,\upsigma}>0

such that if
N_{0}\geq 1
and
N
is sufficiently large in a manner
that depends on
N_{0},A_{*},\mathfrak{D},q,
and
\upsigma
,
and if
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small
(in a manner that depends on
N,N_{0},A_{*},\mathfrak{D},q,
and
\upsigma
),
then the following pointwise estimates hold for
(t,x)\in(T_{\textnormal{Boot}},1]\times\mathbb{T}^{\mathfrak{D}}

\mathfrak{K}_{IJ}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}
and

\displaystyle\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}+1}\sum_{I,J=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}{t}\left|ne_{C}\partial^{\iota}\upgamma_{IJC}-ne_{I}\partial^{\iota}\upgamma_{CJC}-e_{I}\partial^{\iota}e_{J}n\right|(t,x)

\displaystyle\leq Ct^{-1+\upsigma}\mathbb{D}(t){,}

(5.33a)

\displaystyle\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}+1}\sum_{I,J=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}|\mathfrak{K}_{IJ}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}|(t,x)+\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}+1}\sum_{I,J=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}t|\mathfrak{K}_{IJ}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}|(t,x)

.
∎

Remark 5.18

(On the meaning of “Borderline”)
.

Quantities featuring the superscript “Borderline” contain terms that are
either borderline with respect to our low order estimates
or
our high order estimates (or both).
For example, the estimate (
5.33b
) reveals that at the lower orders,

\mathfrak{K}_{IJ}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}
is not a borderline term (see also (
5.48
)),
while the presence of the
C_{*}
-involving term on RHS (
5.35a
)
in Lemma
5.21
below shows that

\mathfrak{K}_{IJ}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}
is indeed borderline in the context
of our top-order energy estimates. Similar remarks apply to other quantities
featuring the superscript “Borderline.”

5.5.9
Differential energy identity for the second fundamental form and connection coefficients

We will derive our top-order energy estimates for the second fundamental form and connection
coefficients by integrating the differential identity provided by the following lemma.

Lemma 5.19

(Top-order differential energy identity for
\{k_{IJ}\}_{I,J=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}

and
\{\upgamma_{IJB}\}_{I,J,B=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}
)
.

Let
\iota
be a top-order spatial multi-index,
i.e.,
|\iota|=N
.
Then for solutions to the
\partial^{\iota}
-commuted equations
(
5.31a
)–(
5.31c
) with
P:=A_{*}+1
,
the following differential energy identity holds, where
the error terms

\mathfrak{K}_{IJ}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}
,

\mathfrak{K}_{IJ}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}
,

\mathfrak{G}_{IJB}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}
,

\mathfrak{G}_{IJB}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}
,

\mathfrak{M}_{I}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}
,
and

\mathfrak{M}_{I}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}

are defined in (
5.32a
)–(
5.32f
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\partial_{t}\left\{(t^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}k_{IJ})(t^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}k_{IJ})\right\}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}\left\{(t^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}\upgamma_{IJB})(t^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}\upgamma_{IJB})\right\}\\
&=\frac{2A_{*}}{t}(t^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}k_{IJ})(t^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}k_{IJ})+\frac{(A_{*}+1)}{t}(t^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}\upgamma_{IJB})(t^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}\upgamma_{IJB})\\
&\ \ +2(t^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}k_{IJ})\left(t^{A_{*}}\mathfrak{K}_{IJ}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}+t^{A_{*}+1}\mathfrak{K}_{IJ}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}\right)\\
&\ \ +(t^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}\upgamma_{IJB})\left(t^{A_{*}+1}\mathfrak{G}_{IJB}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}+t^{A_{*}+1}\mathfrak{G}_{IJB}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}\right)\\
&\ \ +2(t^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}e_{J}n)\left(t^{A_{*}+1}\mathfrak{M}_{J}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}+t^{A_{*}+1}\mathfrak{M}_{J}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}\right)\\
&\ \ +2n(t^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}\upgamma_{CJC})\left(t^{A_{*}+1}\mathfrak{M}_{J}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}+t^{A_{*}+1}\mathfrak{M}_{J}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}\right)\\
&\ \ +2(\partial_{c}e_{I}^{c})(t^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}e_{J}n)(t^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}k_{IJ})\\
&\ \ +2\left\{\partial_{c}(ne_{I}^{c})\right\}(t^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}k_{IJ})(t^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}\upgamma_{CJC})-2\left\{\partial_{c}(ne_{C}^{c})\right\}(t^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}k_{IJ})(t^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}\upgamma_{IJC})\\
&\ \ -2\partial_{c}\left\{t^{2A_{*}+2}e_{I}^{c}(\partial^{\iota}e_{J}n)\partial^{\iota}k_{IJ}\right\}-2\partial_{c}\left\{t^{2A_{*}+2}e_{I}^{c}n(\partial^{\iota}k_{IJ})\partial^{\iota}\upgamma_{CJC}\right\}\\
&\ \ +2\partial_{c}\left\{t^{2A_{*}+2}ne_{C}^{c}(\partial^{\iota}k_{IJ})\partial^{\iota}\upgamma_{IJC}\right\}.\end{split}

(5.34)

Proof.

The proof is a calculation that, although lengthy, is
straightforward; hence, we only explain the main steps.
We first note that
\partial^{\iota}\widetilde{k}_{IJ}=0

and thus we can ignore the formal presence of this term on LHS (
5.31a
).
Next, we expand LHS (
5.34
) using the Leibniz rule.
When
\partial_{t}
falls on
t^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}k_{IJ}
,
we plug in (
5.31a
) with
P:=A_{*}+1
.
When
\partial_{t}
falls on
t^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}\upgamma_{IJB}
,
we plug in (
5.31b
) with
P:=A_{*}+1
.
We then differentiate the resulting terms by parts.
Next, we use the (differentiated) momentum constraint (
5.31c
)
with
P:=A_{*}+1

to substitute for the terms
t^{A_{*}+1}e_{I}\partial^{\iota}k_{IJ}
in the product

2(t^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}e_{J}n)\cdot t^{A_{*}+1}e_{I}\partial^{\iota}k_{IJ}

(which is “present” in the sense that it is needed to cancel a corresponding
product obtained from
expanding the third-to-last term

-2\partial_{c}\left\{t^{2A_{*}+2}e_{I}^{c}(\partial^{\iota}e_{J}n)\partial^{\iota}k_{IJ}\right\}

on RHS (
5.34
)).
Similarly,
we use (
5.31c
)
with
P:=A_{*}+1

to substitute for the terms
t^{A_{*}+1}e_{I}\partial^{\iota}k_{IJ}
in the product

2nt^{A_{*}+1}e_{I}\partial^{\iota}k_{IJ}\cdot t^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}\upgamma_{CJC}

(which is “present” in the sense that it is needed to cancel a corresponding
product obtained from
expanding the next-to-last term

-2\partial_{c}\left\{t^{2A_{*}+2}e_{I}^{c}n(\partial^{\iota}k_{IJ})\partial^{\iota}\upgamma_{CJC}\right\}

on RHS (
5.34
)).
∎

Remark 5.20

(Comments tied to the momentum constraint and well-posedness in CMC-transported spatial coordinates)
.

The (differentiated) momentum constraint (
5.31c
)
plays a crucial role in our proof of Lemma
5.19
;
without this constraint equation, the corresponding differential energy identity would have featured terms
involving one too many derivatives of
k_{IJ}
, which in turn would have led to a fatal loss of one derivative
in the top-order estimates.
An alternate way to overcome the derivative loss
is to use spatial harmonic coordinates on each time slice
\Sigma_{t}
, as in
[
6
]
.
However, such coordinates lead to the presence of a non-zero shift vector in the coordinate expression for the spacetime
metric, and it is not currently known whether the corresponding error terms are compatible with a proof of
stable Big Bang formation. We also emphasize that for similar reasons,
the momentum constraint equation plays a crucial role
in proving local well-posedness for Einstein’s equations in
CMC-transported spatial coordinates; see
[
59
, Theorem 14.1]
.
Moreover, we also highlight that while energy identities such as (
5.34
)
can be used to derive a priori energy estimates for solutions to the
nonlinear

reduced equations
(where by “reduced,” we roughly mean gauge-dependent equations in the spirit of the ones stated in Proposition
2.2
),
the proof of local well-posedness given by
[
59
, Theorem 14.1]

relies on a modified system, which can be shown to be equivalent to the nonlinear reduced equations
(and hence, by the “if and only if” aspect of Proposition
2.2
, equivalent to Einstein’s equations too)
for initial data that satisfy the constraints and the CMC condition (
1.12
) at
t=1
.
The key advantage of the modified system is that
it does not involve constraint equations; this allows one to show that solutions to
linearized versions of the modified system also enjoy good energy estimates,
which is important for the standard iteration/contraction mapping schemes that are used
in proofs of local well-posedness for quasilinear equations.

5.5.10
Control of the error terms in the top-order commuted spatial metric equations

In this section, at the top-order derivative level,
we derive
L^{2}
estimates for the error terms in the equations of
Lemma
5.16
.

Lemma 5.21

(
L^{2}
-control of the error terms in the top-order commuted evolution equations for
k
and
\upgamma
)
.

Recall that
\mathbb{H}_{(\upgamma,k)}
,

\mathbb{H}_{(\psi)}
,
and
\mathbb{D}(t)
are norms from Definition
3.1
,
and assume that the bootstrap assumptions (
3.10
) hold.
Recall that the error terms

\mathfrak{K}_{IJ}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}
,

\mathfrak{K}_{IJ}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}
,

\mathfrak{G}_{IJB}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}
,

\mathfrak{G}_{IJB}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}
,

\mathfrak{M}_{I}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}
,
and

\mathfrak{M}_{I}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}

are defined in (
5.32a
)–(
5.32f
).
There exists a constant
C_{*}>0

independent of
N,N_{0},
and
A_{*}

and a constant
C=C_{N,N_{0},A_{*},\mathfrak{D},q,\upsigma}>0

such that if
N
is sufficiently large in a manner
that depends on
N_{0},A_{*},\mathfrak{D},q,
and
\upsigma
,
and if
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small (in a manner that depends on
N,N_{0},A_{*},\mathfrak{D},q,
and
\upsigma
),
then the following estimates hold for
t\in(T_{\textnormal{Boot}},1]
:

\displaystyle t^{A_{*}}\sqrt{\sum_{|\iota|=N}\sum_{I,J=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}\|\mathfrak{K}_{IJ}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{*}t^{-1}\mathbb{H}_{(\upgamma,k)}(t)+Ct^{-1+\upsigma}\mathbb{D}(t),

(5.35a)

\displaystyle t^{A_{*}+1}\sqrt{\sum_{|\iota|=N}\sum_{I,J,B=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}\|\mathfrak{G}_{IJB}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{*}t^{-1}\mathbb{H}_{(\upgamma,k)}(t)+Ct^{-1+\upsigma}\mathbb{D}(t),

(5.35b)

\displaystyle t^{A_{*}+1}\sqrt{\sum_{|\iota|=N}\sum_{I=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}\|\mathfrak{M}_{I}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{*}t^{-1}\mathbb{H}_{(\upgamma,k)}(t)+C_{*}t^{-1}\mathbb{H}_{(\psi)}(t)+Ct^{-1+\upsigma}\mathbb{D}(t),

(5.35c)

\displaystyle t^{A_{*}+1}\sqrt{\sum_{|\iota|=N}\sum_{I,J=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}\|\mathfrak{K}_{IJ}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq Ct^{-1+\upsigma}\mathbb{D}(t),

(5.36a)

\displaystyle t^{A_{*}+1}\sqrt{\sum_{|\iota|=N}\sum_{I,J,B=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}\|\mathfrak{G}_{IJB}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq Ct^{-1+\upsigma}\mathbb{D}(t),

(5.36b)

\displaystyle t^{A_{*}+1}\sqrt{\sum_{|\iota|=N}\sum_{I=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}\|\mathfrak{M}_{I}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq Ct^{-1+\upsigma}\mathbb{D}(t).

(5.36c)

Proof.

We will give the proofs of (
5.35a
)
and (
5.36a
).
The remaining estimates can be proved using similar arguments, and we omit the details.
To prove (
5.35a
),
we let
\iota
be any spatial multi-index with
|\iota|=N
.
We multiply both sides of (
5.32a
) by
t^{A_{*}}
and take the

\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}
norm.
Using the bootstrap assumptions,
the explicit formulas (
2.58
),
the inequalities in (
3.4
),
Definition
3.1
,
and the product estimate (
4.3
),
we find that

t^{A_{*}}\|\mathfrak{K}_{IJ}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}\leq C_{*}t^{A_{*}-1}\|\partial^{\iota}n\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}+C\varepsilon t^{A_{*}-1}\|n\|_{\dot{H}^{N}(\Sigma_{t})}+C\varepsilon t^{-1+\upsigma}\mathbb{D}(t)
.
We then square this estimate, sum over all
\iota
with
|\iota|=N
,
sum over all
1\leq I,J\leq\mathfrak{D}
, and then take the square root.
We find that

\mbox{LHS~\eqref{E:KTOPORDERBORDERTERMSL2ESTIMATE}}\leq(C_{*}+C\varepsilon)t^{A_{*}-1}\|n\|_{\dot{H}^{N}(\Sigma_{t})}+C\varepsilon t^{-1+\upsigma}\mathbb{D}(t)\leq C_{*}t^{A_{*}-1}\|n\|_{\dot{H}^{N}(\Sigma_{t})}+C\varepsilon t^{-1+\upsigma}\mathbb{D}(t)
.
From this bound and the already derived high order estimate (
5.5b
) for
n
,
we arrive at the desired bound (
5.35a
).

The estimate (
5.36a
)
can be proved by multiplying equation (
5.32b
)
by
t^{A_{*}+1}
and combing arguments similar to the ones we used above
with the estimates of Lemma
4.1
.
∎

5.6
Preliminary identities and inequalities for the scalar field
\psi

This section is an analog of Sect.
5.5

for the scalar field
\psi
. That is,
we derive preliminary low order and high order
identities and inequalities for
\psi
by using the wave equation (
2.24
).
In order to avoid the time derivative of
n
appearing as an error term in the equations
(which would unnecessarily complicate our derivation of the main estimates),
we treat
e_{0}\psi
,
\{e_{I}\psi\}_{I=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}

as separate variables satisfying a first-order system derived from the wave equation,
cf.
[
61
]
.
Roughly, we bound the inhomogeneous terms in the evolution equations
in terms of our solution norms, and we derive an energy identity in differential form.
In Sects.
5.7
–
5.9
,
we will combine these preliminary results with related ones
for
n
,
\{k_{IJ}\}_{I,J=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}
,

\{\upgamma_{IJB}\}_{I,J,B=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}
,

\{e_{I}^{i}\}_{I,i=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}
,
and

\{\upomega_{i}^{I}\}_{I,i=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}

to derive our main a priori estimates,
i.e., to prove Proposition
5.1
.

5.6.1
Commuted evolution equations for
e_{0}\psi
and
e_{I}\psi

In this section, we provide the first-order evolution equations that we will use to control
the scalar functions

e_{0}\psi

and

\{e_{I}\psi\}_{I=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}

as well as their derivatives.

Lemma 5.22

(The first-order evolution system for
e_{0}\psi
,
\{e_{I}\psi\}_{I=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}
, and their derivatives)
.

For solutions to the equations of Proposition
2.2
,
the
{\bf g}
-orthonormal frame derivatives of
\psi
,
namely
e_{0}\psi
and
\{e_{I}\psi\}_{I=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}
,
satisfy the following first-order symmetric hyperbolic system,
where the Kasner background scalars

\widetilde{\psi}=\widetilde{B}\log t

and

\{\widetilde{k}_{IJ}\}_{I,J=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}

are defined in (
2.58
)
(see also Remark
2.6
),
and we recall that
we do not sum over repeated underlined indices:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}[t(e_{0}\psi-\partial_{t}\widetilde{\psi})]

\displaystyle=tne_{C}e_{C}\psi-tn\upgamma_{CCD}e_{D}\psi+t(e_{C}n)e_{C}\psi-(n-1)\partial_{t}\widetilde{\psi}-(n-1)(e_{0}\psi-\partial_{t}\widetilde{\psi}),

(5.37a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{t}e_{I}\psi&=-\frac{\widetilde{q}_{\underline{I}}}{t}e_{\underline{I}}\psi+ne_{I}e_{0}\psi+(n-1)k_{IC}e_{C}\psi+(k_{IC}-\widetilde{k}_{IC})e_{C}\psi+(e_{I}n)\partial_{t}\widetilde{\psi}\\
&\ \ +(e_{I}n)(e_{0}\psi-\partial_{t}\widetilde{\psi}).\end{split}

(5.37b)

Moreover, if
\iota
is a spatial coordinate multi-index
and
P\geq 0
is any real number,
then the following equations hold:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{t}[t^{P}\partial^{\iota}(e_{0}\psi-\partial_{t}\widetilde{\psi})]&=(P-1)[t^{P-1}\partial^{\iota}(e_{0}\psi-\partial_{t}\widetilde{\psi})]+t^{P}ne_{C}\partial^{\iota}e_{C}\psi\\
&\ \ +t^{P-1}\mathfrak{P}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}+t^{P}\mathfrak{P}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)},\end{split}

(5.38a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(t^{P}\partial^{\iota}e_{I}\psi)

\displaystyle=(P-\widetilde{q}_{\underline{I}})t^{P-1}\partial^{\iota}e_{\underline{I}}\psi+t^{P}ne_{I}\partial^{\iota}e_{0}\psi+t^{P}\mathfrak{Q}_{I}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}+t^{P}\mathfrak{Q}_{I}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)},

(5.38b)

where:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{P}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}

\displaystyle:=\partial^{\iota}(n-1)\cdot\partial_{t}\widetilde{\psi}+\sum_{\iota_{1}\cup\iota_{2}=\iota}\partial^{\iota_{1}}(n-1)\cdot\partial^{\iota_{2}}(e_{0}\psi-\partial_{t}\widetilde{\psi}),

(5.39a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathfrak{P}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}&:=\sum_{\iota_{1}\cup\iota_{2}\cup\iota_{3}=\iota,\,|\iota_{3}|<|\iota|}\partial^{\iota_{1}}n\cdot\partial^{\iota_{2}}e\cdot\partial\partial^{\iota_{3}}\vec{e}\psi\\
&\ \ +\sum_{\iota_{1}\cup\iota_{2}\cup\iota_{3}=\iota}\partial^{\iota_{1}}n\cdot\partial^{\iota_{2}}\upgamma\cdot\partial^{\iota_{3}}\vec{e}\psi+\sum_{\iota_{1}\cup\iota_{2}=\iota}\partial^{\iota_{1}}\vec{e}n\cdot\partial^{\iota_{2}}\vec{e}\psi,\end{split}

(5.39b)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Q}_{I}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\iota_{1}\cup\iota_{2}=\iota}\partial^{\iota_{1}}(k-\widetilde{k})\cdot\partial^{\iota_{2}}\vec{e}\psi+\partial^{\iota}\vec{e}n\cdot\partial_{t}\widetilde{\psi}+\sum_{\iota_{1}\cup\iota_{2}=\iota}\partial^{\iota_{1}}\vec{e}n\cdot\partial^{\iota_{2}}(e_{0}\psi-\partial_{t}\widetilde{\psi})

(5.39c)

\displaystyle\ \ +\sum_{\iota_{1}\cup\iota_{2}=\iota,\,|\iota_{2}|<|\iota|}\partial^{\iota_{1}}e\cdot\partial\partial^{\iota_{2}}e_{0}\psi,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Q}_{I}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\iota_{1}\cup\iota_{2}\cup\iota_{3}=\iota,\,|\iota_{3}|<|\iota|}\partial^{\iota_{1}}(n-1)\cdot\partial^{\iota_{2}}e\cdot\partial\partial^{\iota_{3}}e_{0}\psi+\sum_{\iota_{1}\cup\iota_{2}\cup\iota_{3}=\iota}\partial^{\iota_{1}}(n-1)\cdot\partial^{\iota_{2}}k\cdot\partial^{\iota_{3}}\vec{e}\psi.

(5.39d)

Proof.

Equation (
5.37a
) follows from multiplying both sides of (
2.24
) by
nt
,
using that
\partial_{t}(t\partial_{t}\widetilde{\psi})=0
,
and carrying out straightforward algebraic computations.
(
5.37b
) follows from the identity

\partial_{t}=ne_{0}
,
the commutation identity (
4.11
),
and straightforward algebraic computations.
(
5.38a
)–(
5.38b
) then follow from
differentiating (
5.37a
)–(
5.37b
) with
\partial^{\iota}
,
using the Leibniz rule,
multiplying both sides of the resulting equations by
t^{P-1}
and
t^{P}
respectively,
commuting the factors of
t^{P-1}
and
t^{P}
under the operator
\partial_{t}
on the LHSs,
and accounting for the commutators
[t^{P-1},\partial_{t}]
and
[t^{P},\partial_{t}]
.
∎

5.6.2
Pointwise estimates for the error terms in the scalar field evolution equations

In this section,
we derive the pointwise estimates for the error terms in the equations of
Lemma
5.22

that we will later use to control
e_{0}\psi-\partial_{t}\widetilde{\psi}

at derivative levels
\leq N_{0}+1

and

e_{I}\psi

at derivative levels
\leq N_{0}
.

Lemma 5.23

(Pointwise estimates for the error terms in the evolution equations for

\partial^{\leq N_{0}+1}(e_{0}\psi-\partial_{t}\widetilde{\psi})
and

\{\partial^{\leq N_{0}}e_{I}\psi\}_{I=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}
)
.

Recall that
\mathbb{D}(t)
is the total norm of the dynamic variables from Definition
3.1
,
and assume that the bootstrap assumptions (
3.10
) hold.
Recall that the error terms

\mathfrak{P}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}
,

\mathfrak{P}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}
,

\mathfrak{Q}_{I}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}
,
and

\mathfrak{Q}_{I}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}

are defined in (
5.39a
)–(
5.39d
).
There exists a constant
C=C_{N,N_{0},A_{*},\mathfrak{D},q,\upsigma}>0

such that if
N
is sufficiently large in a manner
that depends on
N_{0},A_{*},\mathfrak{D},q,
and
\upsigma
,
and if
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small (in a manner that depends on
N,N_{0},A_{*},\mathfrak{D},q,
and
\upsigma
),
then the following pointwise
estimates hold for
(t,x)\in(T_{\textnormal{Boot}},1]\times\mathbb{T}^{\mathfrak{D}}
:

\displaystyle\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}+1}t|ne_{C}\partial^{\iota}e_{C}\psi|(t,x)

\displaystyle\leq Ct^{-1+\upsigma}\mathbb{D}(t),

(5.40a)

\displaystyle\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}+1}|\mathfrak{P}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}|(t,x)+\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}+1}t|\mathfrak{P}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}|(t,x)

\displaystyle\leq Ct^{-1+\upsigma}\mathbb{D}(t),

(5.40b)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}}\sum_{I=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}t^{q}\left|ne_{I}\partial^{\iota}e_{0}\psi\right|(t,x)&\leq C\varepsilon\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}}\sum_{I,i=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}t^{q-1}|\partial^{\iota}(e_{I}^{i}-\widetilde{e}_{I}^{i})|(t,x)\\
&\ \ +Ct^{-1+\upsigma}\mathbb{D}(t),\end{split}

(5.40c)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}}\sum_{I=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}t^{q}|\mathfrak{Q}_{I}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}|(t,x)&\leq C\varepsilon\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}}\sum_{I=1,\cdot,\mathfrak{D}}t^{q-1}|\partial^{\iota}e_{I}\psi|(t,x)\\
&\ \ +C\varepsilon\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}}\sum_{I,i=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}t^{q-1}|\partial^{\iota}(e_{I}^{i}-\widetilde{e}_{I}^{i})|(t,x)\\
&\ \ +Ct^{-1+\upsigma}\mathbb{D}(t),\end{split}

(5.40d)

\displaystyle\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}}\sum_{I=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}t^{q}|\mathfrak{Q}_{I}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}|(t,x)

\displaystyle\leq Ct^{-1+\upsigma}\mathbb{D}(t).

(5.40e)

Proof.

We apply the same arguments we used in the proof of
Lemmas
5.10

and
Lemma
5.17
,
taking into account the structure of the terms
on RHS (
5.39a
)–(
5.39d
)
and the fact that the low order norm (
3.9a
)
controls
e_{0}\psi
at up to derivative level
N_{0}+1

(in particular, we use this fact to derive
(
5.40b
)–(
5.40c
)).
We also clarify that to obtain (
5.40c
),
we use the triangle inequality to bound the summand on the LHS by

\leq t^{q}|n(e_{I}^{c}-\widetilde{e}_{I}^{c})\partial_{c}\partial^{\iota}e_{0}\psi|+t^{q}|n\widetilde{e}_{I}^{c}\partial_{c}\partial^{\iota}e_{0}\psi|

and then bound (rather inefficiently) the first product by the term

C\varepsilon\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}}\sum_{I,i=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}t^{q-1}|\partial^{\iota}(e_{I}^{i}-\widetilde{e}_{I}^{i})|(t,x)

on RHS (
5.40c
)
and, with the help of (
2.58
) and (
3.4
), the second product by the term

Ct^{-1+\upsigma}\mathbb{D}(t)

on RHS (
5.40c
).
∎

5.6.3
Differential energy identity for the scalar field

We will derive our top-order energy estimates for the scalar field by integrating the differential identity provided by the following lemma.

Lemma 5.24

(Top-order differential energy identity for
e_{0}\psi
and
\{e_{I}\psi\}_{I=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}
)
.

Let
\iota
be a top-order spatial multi-index,
i.e.,
|\iota|=N
.
Then for solutions to the
\partial^{\iota}
-commuted equations
(
5.38a
)–(
5.38b
)
with
P:=A_{*}+1
,
the following differential energy identity holds,
where the error terms

\mathfrak{P}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}
,

\mathfrak{P}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}
,

\mathfrak{Q}_{I}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}
,
and

\mathfrak{Q}_{I}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}

are defined in (
5.39a
)–(
5.39d
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\partial_{t}\left\{(t^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}e_{0}\psi)^{2}\right\}+\partial_{t}\left\{(t^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}e_{I}\psi)(t^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}e_{I}\psi)\right\}\\
&=\frac{2A_{*}}{t}(t^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}e_{0}\psi)^{2}+\frac{2(A_{*}+1-\widetilde{q}_{I})}{t}(t^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}e_{I}\psi)(t^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}e_{I}\psi)\\
&\ \ +2(t^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}e_{0}\psi)\left(t^{A_{*}}\mathfrak{P}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}+t^{A_{*}+1}\mathfrak{P}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}\right)\\
&\ \ +2(t^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}e_{I}\psi)\left(t^{A_{*}+1}\mathfrak{Q}_{I}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}+t^{A_{*}+1}\mathfrak{Q}_{I}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}\right)\\
&\ \ -2\left\{\partial_{c}(ne_{C}^{c})\right\}(t^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}e_{C}\psi)(t^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}e_{0}\psi)+2t^{2A_{*}+2}\partial_{c}\left\{ne_{C}^{c}(\partial^{\iota}e_{C}\psi)(\partial^{\iota}e_{0}\psi)\right\}.\end{split}

(5.41)

Proof.

This lemma follows from straightforward calculation, so we only explain the main steps.
We first note that
\partial^{\iota}\partial_{t}\widetilde{\psi}=0

and thus we can ignore the formal presence of this term on LHS (
5.38a
).
Next, we expand LHS (
5.41
) using the Leibniz rule.
When
\partial_{t}
falls on
t^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}e_{0}\psi
,
we plug in (
5.38a
) with
P:=A_{*}+1
.
When
\partial_{t}
falls on
t^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}e_{I}\psi
,
we plug in (
5.38b
) with
P:=A_{*}+1
.
Also differentiating by parts,
we arrive at the desired identity (
5.41
).
∎

5.6.4
Control of the error terms in the top-order commuted scalar field evolution equations

In this section, at the top-order derivative level,
we derive
L^{2}
estimates for the error terms in the evolution equations of
Lemma
5.22
.

Lemma 5.25

(
L^{2}
-control of the error terms in the top-order commuted scalar field equations)
.

Recall that

\mathbb{H}_{(\upgamma,k)}
,

\mathbb{H}_{(\psi)}
,
and
\mathbb{D}(t)
are norms from Definition
3.1
,
and assume that the bootstrap assumptions (
3.10
) hold.
Recall that the error terms

\mathfrak{P}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}
,

\mathfrak{P}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}
,

\mathfrak{Q}_{I}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}
,
and

\mathfrak{Q}_{I}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}

are defined in (
5.39a
)–(
5.39d
).
There exists a constant
C_{*}>0

independent of
N,N_{0},
and
A_{*}

and a constant
C=C_{N,N_{0},A_{*},\mathfrak{D},q,\upsigma}>0

such that if
N
is sufficiently large in a manner
that depends on
N_{0},A_{*},\mathfrak{D},q,
and
\upsigma
,
and if
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small (in a manner that depends on
N,N_{0},A_{*},\mathfrak{D},q,
and
\upsigma
),
then the following estimates hold for
t\in(T_{\textnormal{Boot}},1]
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&t^{A_{*}}\sqrt{\sum_{|\iota|=N}\|\mathfrak{P}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}^{2}}+t^{A_{*}+1}\sqrt{\sum_{|\iota|=N}\sum_{I=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}\|\mathfrak{Q}_{I}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}^{2}}\\
&\leq C_{*}t^{-1}\mathbb{H}_{(\upgamma,k)}(t)+C_{*}t^{-1}\mathbb{H}_{(\psi)}(t)+Ct^{-1+\upsigma}\mathbb{D}(t),\end{split}

(5.42a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&t^{A_{*}+1}\sqrt{\sum_{|\iota|=N}\|\mathfrak{P}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}^{2}}+t^{A_{*}+1}\sqrt{\sum_{|\iota|=N}\sum_{I=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}\|\mathfrak{Q}_{I}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}^{2}}\\
&\leq Ct^{-1+\upsigma}\mathbb{D}(t).\end{split}

(5.42b)

Proof.

We apply the same arguments that we used
in the proof of Lemma
5.21

to the terms on RHSs (
5.39a
)–(
5.39d
).
∎

5.7
Integral inequality for the low order solution norms

In the next proposition, we combine some of the results derived earlier in Sect.
5

to obtain an integral inequality for the low order solution norms.
In Sect.
5.8
, we will derive
a related integral inequality for the high order solution norms.
Then, in Sect.
5.9
,
we will combine the two integral inequalities
and carry out the proof of our main a priori estimates.

Proposition 5.26

(Integral inequality for the low order solution norms)
.

Recall that
\mathbb{L}_{(e,\upomega,\upgamma,k,\psi)}(t)
is a low order norm and that

\mathbb{D}(t)
is the total norm of the dynamic solution variables
(see Definition
3.1
).
Under the assumptions of Proposition
5.1
,
including the bootstrap assumptions (
3.10
),
there exists a constant
C=C_{N,N_{0},A_{*},\mathfrak{D},q,\upsigma}>0

such that if
N_{0}\geq 1
and
N
is sufficiently large in a manner
that depends on
N_{0},A_{*},\mathfrak{D},q,
and
\upsigma
,
and if
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small (in a manner that depends on
N,N_{0},A_{*},\mathfrak{D},q,
and
\upsigma
),
then the following estimate holds for
t\in(T_{\textnormal{Boot}},1]
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{(e,\upomega,\upgamma,k,\psi)}^{2}(t)

\displaystyle\leq C\mathbb{L}_{(e,\upomega,\upgamma,k,\psi)}^{2}(1)+C\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1+\upsigma}\mathbb{D}^{2}(s)\,ds.

(5.43)

Proof.

The polarized
U(1)
-symmetric case will require an additional observation, which we provide at the end of the proof.

The proof except for the polarized
U(1)
-symmetric case.
Recall (
5.22
) and Remark
1.2
.
We define the scalar function
Q(t,x)\geq 0

as follows, where the background Kasner scalars are defined in
Sect.
2.3
and we suppress the
(t,x)

arguments on RHS (
5.44
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}Q^{2}=Q^{2}(t,x)&:=\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}}\mathop{\sum_{I,J,B=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}}_{I<J}\left[t^{q}\partial^{\iota}(\upgamma_{IJB}+\upgamma_{JBI})\right]^{2}+\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}+1}\sum_{I,J=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}\left[t\partial^{\iota}(k_{IJ}-\widetilde{k}_{IJ})\right]^{2}\\
&\ \ +\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}}\sum_{I,i=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}\left[t^{q}\partial^{\iota}(e_{I}^{i}-\widetilde{e}_{I}^{i})\right]^{2}+\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}}\sum_{I,i=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}\left[t^{q}\partial^{\iota}(\upomega_{i}^{I}-\widetilde{\upomega}_{i}^{I})\right]^{2}\\
&\ \ +\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}+1}\left[t\partial^{\iota}(e_{0}\psi-\partial_{t}\widetilde{\psi})\right]^{2}+\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}}\sum_{I=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}[t^{q}\partial^{\iota}e_{I}\psi]^{2}.\end{split}

(5.44)

Throughout the proof,
we will silently use the estimates

C^{-1}\|Q\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{t})}\leq\mathbb{L}_{(e,\upomega,\upgamma,k,\psi)}(t)\leq C\|Q\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{t})}

and
\|Q\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{t})}\leq C\mathbb{D}(t)
,
which follow easily from the definitions of the quantities involved
and the identity (
5.22
).
In particular, to prove (
5.43
),
it suffices to derive the following pointwise bound for
Q^{2}(t,x)
:

\displaystyle Q^{2}(t,x)

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{L}_{(e,\upomega,\upgamma,k,\psi)}^{2}(1)+\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1+\upsigma}\mathbb{D}^{2}(s)\,ds.

(5.45)

To prove (
5.45
),
we will derive the following pointwise bound for
(t,x)\in(T_{\textnormal{Boot}},1]\times\mathbb{T}^{\mathfrak{D}}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}Q^{2}(t,x)&\leq C\mathbb{L}_{(e,\upomega,\upgamma,k,\psi)}^{2}(1)+(C\varepsilon-4\upsigma)\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1}\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}}\mathop{\sum_{I,J,B=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}}_{I<J}\left[s^{q}\partial^{\iota}(\upgamma_{IJB}+\upgamma_{JBI})(s,x)\right]^{2}\,ds\\
&\ \ +(C\varepsilon-4\upsigma)\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1}\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}}\sum_{I=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}\left[s^{q}\partial^{\iota}e_{I}\psi(s,x)\right]^{2}\,ds\\
&\ \ +(C\varepsilon-4\upsigma)\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1}\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}}\sum_{I,i=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}\left[s^{q}\partial^{\iota}(e_{I}^{i}-\widetilde{e}_{I}^{i})(s,x)\right]^{2}\,ds\\
&\ \ +(C\varepsilon-4\upsigma)\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1}\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}}\sum_{I,i=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}\left[s^{q}\partial^{\iota}(\upomega_{i}^{I}-\widetilde{\upomega}_{i}^{I})(s,x)\right]^{2}\,ds\\
&\ \ +C\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1+\upsigma}\mathbb{D}^{2}(s)\,ds.\end{split}

(5.46)

Then for
\varepsilon
sufficiently small, the first four integrals on RHS (
5.46
) are
negative, and we can discard them; the desired bound (
5.45
) then follows.

It remains for us to prove (
5.46
).
We will show that the following pointwise estimates hold
for
(t,x)\in(T_{\textnormal{Boot}},1]\times\mathbb{T}^{\mathfrak{D}}
,
where to condense the notation, we omit the arguments

(t,x)
on the LHSs and the integrand arguments
(s,x)

on the RHSs:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}}\mathop{\sum_{I,J,B=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}}_{I<J}\left[t^{q}\partial^{\iota}(\upgamma_{IJB}+\upgamma_{JBI})\right]^{2}&\leq C\mathbb{L}_{(\upgamma,k)}^{2}(1)\\
&\ \ +(C\varepsilon-4\upsigma)\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1}\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}}\mathop{\sum_{I,J,B=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}}_{I<J}\left[s^{q}\partial^{\iota}(\upgamma_{IJB}+\upgamma_{JBI})\right]^{2}\,ds\\
&\ \ +C\varepsilon\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1}\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}}\sum_{I,i=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}\left[s^{q}\partial^{\iota}(e_{I}^{i}-\widetilde{e}_{I}^{i})\right]^{2}\,ds\\
&\ \ +C\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1+\upsigma}\mathbb{D}^{2}(s)\,ds,\end{split}

(5.47)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}+1}\sum_{I,J=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}\left[t\partial^{\iota}(k_{IJ}-\widetilde{k}_{IJ})\right]^{2}&\leq C\mathbb{L}_{(\upgamma,k)}^{2}(1)\\
&\ \ +C\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1+\upsigma}\mathbb{D}^{2}(s)\,ds,\end{split}

(5.48)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}}\sum_{I,i=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}\left[t^{q}\partial^{\iota}(e_{I}^{i}-\widetilde{e}_{I}^{i})\right]^{2}&\leq C\mathbb{L}_{(e,\upomega)}^{2}(1)\\
&\ \ +(C\varepsilon-4\upsigma)\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1}\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}}\sum_{I,i=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}\left[s^{q}\partial^{\iota}(e_{I}^{i}-\widetilde{e}_{I}^{i})\right]^{2}\,ds\\
&\ \ +C\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1+\upsigma}\mathbb{D}^{2}(s)\,ds,\end{split}

(5.49)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}}\sum_{I,i=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}\left[t^{q}\partial^{\iota}(\upomega_{i}^{I}-\widetilde{\upomega}_{i}^{I})\right]^{2}&\leq C\mathbb{L}_{(e,\upomega)}^{2}(1)\\
&\ \ +(C\varepsilon-4\upsigma)\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1}\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}}\sum_{I,i=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}\left[s^{q}\partial^{\iota}(\upomega_{i}^{I}-\widetilde{\upomega}_{i}^{I})\right]^{2}\,ds\\
&\ \ +C\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1+\upsigma}\mathbb{D}^{2}(s)\,ds,\end{split}

(5.50)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}+1}\left[t\partial^{\iota}(e_{0}\psi-\partial_{t}\widetilde{\psi})\right]^{2}&\leq C\mathbb{L}_{(\psi)}^{2}(1)\\
&\ \ +C\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1+\upsigma}\mathbb{D}^{2}(s)\,ds,\end{split}

(5.51)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}}\sum_{I=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}[t^{q}\partial^{\iota}e_{I}\psi]^{2}&\leq C\mathbb{L}_{(\psi)}^{2}(1)\\
&\ \ +(C\varepsilon-4\upsigma)\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1}\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}}\sum_{I=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}[s^{q}\partial^{\iota}e_{I}\psi]^{2}\,ds\\
&\ \ +C\varepsilon\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1}\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}}\sum_{I,i=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}\left[s^{q}\partial^{\iota}(e_{I}^{i}-\widetilde{e}_{I}^{i})\right]^{2}\,ds\\
&\ \ +C\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1+\upsigma}\mathbb{D}^{2}(s)\,ds.\end{split}

(5.52)

Then adding (
5.47
)–(
5.52
),
we arrive at (
5.46
).

To prove (
5.47
), we first multiply
equation (
5.20
)
by
2[t^{q}\partial^{\iota}(\upgamma_{IJB}+\upgamma_{JBI})]

to obtain the evolution equation

\partial_{t}\left\{[t^{q}\partial^{\iota}(\upgamma_{IJB}+\upgamma_{JBI})]^{2}\right\}=2[t^{q}\partial^{\iota}(\upgamma_{IJB}+\upgamma_{JBI})]\times\mbox{RHS~\eqref{E:SCHEMATICSTRUCTURECOEFFICIENTEVOLUTIONEQUATION}}
.
We then integrate this equation in time over
[t,1]
with respect to
ds
,
apply the fundamental theorem of calculus,
and then sum the resulting identity
over all
\iota
with
|\iota|\leq N_{0}

and over all

I,J,B=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}
with
I<J
.
In the resulting identity, we place the term

\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}}\underset{I<J}{\underset{I,J,B=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}{\sum}}\left[t^{q}\partial^{\iota}(\upgamma_{IJB}+\upgamma_{JBI})(t,x)\right]^{2}

on the left-hand side (as the only term on LHS (
5.47
)),
while the resulting initial data term (on
\Sigma_{1}
)
is
\leq
the term
C\mathbb{L}_{(\upgamma,k)}^{2}(1)
on RHS (
5.47
).
Next, noting that the first term

\left\{q-(\widetilde{q}_{\underline{I}}+\widetilde{q}_{\underline{J}}-\widetilde{q}_{\underline{B}})\right\}t^{q-1}\partial^{\iota}(\upgamma_{\underline{I}\underline{J}\underline{B}}+\upgamma_{\underline{J}\underline{B}\underline{I}})

on RHS (
5.20
)
generates the integrals

-2\left\{q-(\widetilde{q}_{\underline{I}}+\widetilde{q}_{\underline{J}}-\widetilde{q}_{\underline{B}})\right\}\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1}\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}}\underset{I<J}{\underset{I,J,B=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}{\sum}}[s^{q}\partial^{\iota}(\upgamma_{IJB}+\upgamma_{JBI})]^{2}\,ds

(where the overall minus sign in front of these integrals
is correct because
t\in(T_{\textnormal{Boot}},1]

and
\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}}\underset{I<J}{\underset{I,J,B=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}{\sum}}\left[t^{q}\partial^{\iota}(\upgamma_{IJB}+\upgamma_{JBI})(t,x)\right]^{2}
is on LHS (
5.47
)),
we can use (
3.4
) to bound these integrals
by
\leq
the negative-definite term

-4\upsigma\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1}\sum_{|\iota|\leq N_{0}}\underset{I<J}{\underset{I,J,B=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}{\sum}}[s^{q}\partial^{\iota}(\upgamma_{IJB}+\upgamma_{JBI})]^{2}\,ds

on RHS (
5.47
).
Finally, with the help of the identity (
5.22
),
the error term estimates
(
5.23a
)–(
5.23b
),
and Young’s inequality,
we see that the terms generated by the remaining terms
on RHS (
5.20
)
are
\leq
the sum of the remaining terms
on RHS (
5.47
)
as desired.

The estimate (
5.48
) follows
from a similar argument based on equation (
5.31a
)
with
P:=1
and
|\iota|\leq N_{0}+1

and the error term estimates
(
5.33a
)–(
5.33b
).

The estimate (
5.49
) follows
from a similar argument based on equation (
5.27a
)
with
P:=q

and the error term estimates
(
5.29a
)–(
5.29b
).
The estimate (
5.50
)
can be proved via similar arguments based on equation (
5.27b
)
with
P:=q

and the error term estimates
(
5.29c
)–(
5.29d
).

The estimate (
5.51
) follows
from a similar argument based on equation (
5.38a
)
with
P:=1
and
|\iota|\leq N_{0}+1

and the error term estimates
(
5.40a
)–(
5.40b
).

Finally, the estimate (
5.52
) follows
from a similar argument based on equation (
5.38b
)
with
P:=q

and the error term estimates
(
5.40c
)–(
5.40e
).
This completes the proof except in the polarized
U(1)
-symmetric case.

The proof in the polarized
U(1)
-symmetric case.

By (
5.25
),
in polarized
U(1)
-symmetry with
\mathfrak{D}=3
,
the structure coefficient
\upgamma_{IJB}+\upgamma_{JBI}

vanishes unless
I=B\neq J

(in which case (
2.20
) implies
\upgamma_{IJB}+\upgamma_{JBI}=\upgamma_{IJB}
– though this identity is not needed
for our results)
or
B=J\neq I
.
The key point is that for the non-zero structure coefficients, when
I=B
,
the factor
\frac{\widetilde{q}_{\underline{I}}+\widetilde{q}_{\underline{J}}-\widetilde{q}_{\underline{B}}}{t}

on LHS (
5.19
)
reduces to

\frac{\widetilde{q}_{\underline{J}}}{t}
,
and similarly, when
B=J
,
the factor
\frac{\widetilde{q}_{\underline{I}}+\widetilde{q}_{\underline{J}}-\widetilde{q}_{\underline{B}}}{t}

on LHS (
5.19
)
reduces to

\frac{\widetilde{q}_{\underline{I}}}{t}
.
Hence, using the definition (
3.4
) of
q

in the polarized
U(1)
-symmetric case, we can repeat the proof of (
5.47
)
given above in the non-symmetric case
– but making the change

\frac{(\widetilde{q}_{\underline{I}}+\widetilde{q}_{\underline{J}}-\widetilde{q}_{\underline{B}})}{t}\rightarrow\frac{\widetilde{q}_{\underline{J}}}{t}

or

\frac{(\widetilde{q}_{\underline{I}}+\widetilde{q}_{\underline{J}}-\widetilde{q}_{\underline{B}})}{t}\rightarrow\frac{\widetilde{q}_{\underline{I}}}{t}

in the relevant spots – to derive the desired estimates.
∎

5.8
Integral inequality for the high order solution norms

In the next proposition, we combine some of the results derived earlier in Sect.
5

to obtain an integral inequality for the high order solution norms.

Proposition 5.27

(Top-order energy integral inequalities)
.

Recall that
\mathbb{H}_{(\upgamma,k)}
,

\mathbb{H}_{(\psi)}
,

\mathbb{H}_{(e,\upomega)}
,
and
\mathbb{D}(t)
are norms from Definition
3.1
.
Under the assumptions of Proposition
5.1
,
including the bootstrap assumptions (
3.10
),
there exists a constant
C_{*}>0

independent of
N,N_{0},
and
A_{*}

and a constant
C=C_{N,N_{0},A_{*},\mathfrak{D},q,\upsigma}>0

such that if
N_{0}\geq 1
and
N
is sufficiently large in a manner
that depends on
N_{0},A_{*},\mathfrak{D},q,
and
\upsigma
,
and if
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small (in a manner that depends on
N,N_{0},A_{*},\mathfrak{D},q,
and
\upsigma
),
then the following estimates hold for
t\in(T_{\textnormal{Boot}},1]
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{H}_{(\upgamma,k)}^{2}(t)&\leq C\mathbb{H}_{(\upgamma,k)}^{2}(1)\\
&\ \ +(C_{*}-A_{*})\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1}\mathbb{H}_{(\upgamma,k)}^{2}(s)\,ds+C_{*}\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1}\mathbb{H}_{(\psi)}^{2}(s)\,ds+C\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1+\upsigma}\mathbb{D}^{2}(s)\,ds,\end{split}

(5.53a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{H}_{(\psi)}^{2}(t)&\leq C\mathbb{H}_{(\psi)}^{2}(1)\\
&\ \ +C_{*}\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1}\mathbb{H}_{(\upgamma,k)}^{2}(s)\,ds+(C_{*}-A_{*})\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1}\mathbb{H}_{(\psi)}^{2}(s)\,ds+C\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1+\upsigma}\mathbb{D}^{2}(s)\,ds,\end{split}

(5.53b)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{H}_{(e,\upomega)}^{2}(t)&\leq C\mathbb{H}_{(e,\upomega)}^{2}(1)\\
&\ \ +C_{*}\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1}\mathbb{H}_{(\upgamma,k)}^{2}(s)\,ds+(C_{*}-A_{*})\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1}\mathbb{H}_{(e,\upomega)}^{2}(s)\,ds+C\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1+\upsigma}\mathbb{D}^{2}(s)\,ds.\end{split}

(5.53c)

Proof.

We stress that throughout the proof,
C
and
C_{*}

denote constants that have the properties stated in Sect.
1.12
,
and that these constants can vary from line to line. In particular,
the “final constants” appearing in
(
5.53a
)–(
5.53c
)
do not have to coincide with the constants appearing in the proof.

To prove (
5.53a
),
we first integrate the differential energy identity (
5.34
)
over
[t,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{\mathfrak{D}}
with respect to
ds\,dx
,
note that the integrals of the last three (perfect-spatial-derivative)
terms on RHS (
5.34
) vanish,
sum the resulting identity over all
\iota
with
|\iota|=N
,
use (
5.5a
) to control the top-order derivatives
of the lapse,
use the estimates

\|n-1\|_{W^{1,\infty}(\Sigma_{t})}\lesssim t^{\upsigma}

and
\|e\|_{W^{1,\infty}(\Sigma_{t})}\lesssim t^{-1+2\upsigma}

(which are simple consequences of
(
2.58
),
the inequalities in (
3.4
),
and the bootstrap assumptions),
and use the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality for integrals and sums
and Young’s inequality
to deduce that the following estimate holds for
t\in(T_{Boot)},1]
,
where
C_{*}>0
and
C>0
are as in the statement of the proposition:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sum_{|\iota|=N}\sum_{I,J=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}t^{2A_{*}+2}\|\partial^{\iota}k_{IJ}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}t^{2A_{*}+2}\sum_{|\iota|=N}\sum_{I,J,B=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}\|\partial^{\iota}\upgamma_{IJB}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}^{2}\\
&\leq C\mathbb{H}_{(\upgamma,k)}^{2}(1)\\
&\ \ +(C_{*}-A_{*})\int_{t}^{1}\left\{\sum_{|\iota|=N}\sum_{I,J=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}s^{2A_{*}+1}\|\partial^{\iota}k_{IJ}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{s})}^{2}+\sum_{|\iota|=N}\sum_{I,J,B=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}s^{2A_{*}+1}\|\partial^{\iota}\upgamma_{IJB}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{s})}^{2}\right\}\,ds\\
&\ \ +\sum_{|\iota|=N}\sum_{I,J=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}\int_{t}^{1}s^{2A_{*}+1}\left\|\mathfrak{K}_{IJ}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}\right\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{s})}^{2}\,ds\\
&\ \ +\sum_{|\iota|=N}\sum_{I,J,B=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}\int_{t}^{1}s^{2A_{*}+3}\left\|\mathfrak{G}_{IJB}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}\right\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{s})}^{2}\,ds\\
&\ \ +\sum_{|\iota|=N}\sum_{J=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}\int_{t}^{1}s^{2A_{*}+3}\left\|\mathfrak{M}_{J}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}\right\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{s})}^{2}\,ds\\
&\ \ +C\sum_{|\iota|=N}\sum_{I,J=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}\int_{t}^{1}\|s^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}k_{IJ}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{s})}\left\|s^{A_{*}+1}\mathfrak{K}_{IJ}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}\right\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{s})}\,ds\\
&\ \ +C\sum_{|\iota|=N}\sum_{I,J,B=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}\int_{t}^{1}\|s^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}\upgamma_{IJB}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{s})}\left\|s^{A_{*}+1}\mathfrak{G}_{IJB}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}\right\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{s})}\,ds\\
&\ \ +C\sum_{|\iota|=N}\sum_{I,J,B,E=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}\int_{t}^{1}\left\{\|s^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}\upgamma_{IJB}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{s})}+s^{\upsigma}\mathbb{D}(s)\right\}\left\|s^{A_{*}+1}\mathfrak{M}_{E}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}\right\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{s})}\,ds\\
&\ \ +C\sum_{|\iota|=N}\sum_{I,J,B,E,F=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1+\upsigma}\|s^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}k_{IJ}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{s})}\left\{\|s^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}\upgamma_{BEF}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{s})}+s^{\upsigma}\mathbb{D}(s)\right\}\,ds\\
&\ \ +C\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1+\upsigma}\mathbb{D}^{2}(s)\,ds.\end{split}

(5.54)

Using Lemma
5.21
,
we deduce that the three integrals involving
the borderline terms

\mathfrak{K}_{IJ}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}
,

\mathfrak{G}_{IJB}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}
,
and
\mathfrak{M}_{J}^{(\textnormal{Border};\iota)}

are bounded by:

\displaystyle\leq C_{*}\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1}\left\{\mathbb{H}_{(\upgamma,k)}^{2}(s)+\mathbb{H}_{(\psi)}^{2}(s)\right\}\,ds+C\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1+\upsigma}\mathbb{D}^{2}(s)\,ds,

and that (in view of Definition
3.1
)
the three integrals involving
the terms
\mathfrak{K}_{IJ}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}
,

\mathfrak{G}_{IJB}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}
,
and
\mathfrak{M}_{J}^{(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)}

are bounded by

\leq C\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1+\upsigma}\mathbb{D}^{2}(s)\,ds
.
Moreover, appealing to Definition
3.1
,
we see that the integrals

C\sum_{|\iota|=N}\sum_{I,J,B,E,F=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1+\upsigma}\|s^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}k_{IJ}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{s})}\left\{\|s^{A_{*}+1}\partial^{\iota}\upgamma_{BEF}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{s})}+s^{\upsigma}\mathbb{D}(s)\right\}\,ds

on the next-to-last line of RHS (
5.54
)
are bounded by

\leq C\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1+\upsigma}\mathbb{D}^{2}(s)\,ds
.
From these estimates,
we arrive,
in view of Definition
3.1
,
at the desired estimate (
5.53a
).

The inequality (
5.53b
)
follows from a similar argument based on the
scalar field differential energy identity (
5.41
)
and the error term estimates of Lemma
5.25
;
we omit the details.

To prove (
5.53c
),
we first set
P:=A_{*}+q
in equation (
5.27a
)
and multiply it by
2[t^{A_{*}+q}\partial^{\iota}({e_{I}^{i}}-\widetilde{e}_{I}^{i})]

to deduce:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{t}\left\{\left[t^{A_{*}+q}\partial^{\iota}({e_{I}^{i}}-\widetilde{e}_{I}^{i})\right]^{2}\right\}&=\frac{2(A_{*}+q-\widetilde{q}_{\underline{I}})}{t}\left[t^{A_{*}+q}\partial^{\iota}({e_{\underline{I}}^{i}}-\widetilde{e}_{\underline{I}}^{i})\right]^{2}\\
&\ \ +2(t^{A_{*}+q}\mathfrak{E}_{\underline{I}}^{\underline{i};(\textnormal{Border};\iota)})\left[t^{A_{*}+q}\partial^{\iota}({e_{\underline{I}}^{\underline{i}}}-\widetilde{e}_{\underline{I}}^{\underline{i}})\right]\\
&\ \ +2(t^{A_{*}+q}\mathfrak{E}_{\underline{I}}^{\underline{i};(\textnormal{Junk};\iota)})\left[t^{A_{*}+q}\partial^{\iota}({e_{\underline{I}}^{\underline{i}}}-\widetilde{e}_{\underline{I}}^{\underline{i}})\right].\end{split}

(5.55)

We then argue as in the proof of (
5.53a
),
but using (
5.55
)
in place of (
5.34
)
and the error term estimates of Lemma
5.15

in place of those of Lemma
5.21
.
Summing the resulting inequality over
I,i=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}
and
also noting that
C\varepsilon\leq C_{*}
, we deduce
that the following estimate holds for
t\in(T_{Boot)},1]
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}t^{2(A_{*}+q)}\|e\|_{\dot{H}^{N}(\Sigma_{t})}^{2}&\leq\|e\|_{\dot{H}^{N}(\Sigma_{1})}^{2}+C_{*}\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1}\mathbb{H}_{(\upgamma,k)}^{2}(s)\,ds+C_{*}\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1}\mathbb{H}_{(e,\upomega)}^{2}(s)\,ds\\
&\ \ -A_{*}\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1}\left\{s^{2(A_{*}+q)}\|e\|_{\dot{H}^{N}(\Sigma_{s})}^{2}\right\}\,ds+C\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1+\upsigma}\mathbb{D}^{2}(s)\,ds.\end{split}

(5.56)

Next, we note that the one-form components
\{\upomega_{i}^{I}\}_{I,i=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}

satisfy the same inequality, that is, (
5.56
)
holds with
\upomega
in place of
e
; to see this, one argues as in the proof of
(
5.56
), but using the
evolution equation (
5.27b
) with
P:=A_{*}+q

and the last two error term estimates in Lemma
5.15
.
Adding this top-order energy inequality
for the
\{\upomega_{i}^{I}\}_{I,i=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}

to the inequality
(
5.56
),
and considering the definition (
3.9b
)
of
\mathbb{H}_{(e,\upomega)}(t)
,
we arrive at the desired estimate (
5.53c
).
We have therefore proved the proposition.
∎

5.9
Proof of Proposition
5.1

We start by adding the integral inequalities
(
5.43
)
and
(
5.53a
)–(
5.53c
)
to obtain, in view of Definition
3.1
and (
5.1
),
the following inequality for
t\in(T_{\textnormal{Boot}},1]
,
valid under largeness/smallness assumptions on the parameters that we describe just below
(and we again stress that constants labeled “
C_{*}
” – though we allow them
to vary from line to line – are always independent of
N_{0},N
and
A_{*}
):

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}^{2}(t)

\displaystyle\leq C\mathring{\upepsilon}^{2}+(C_{*}-A_{*})\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1}\mathbb{H}_{(e,\upomega,\upgamma,k,\psi)}^{2}(s)\,ds+C\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1+\upsigma}\mathbb{D}^{2}(s)\,ds.

(5.57)

We now fix
A_{*}
to be sufficiently large so that
the factor
C_{*}-A_{*}
on RHS (
5.57
) is negative.
For this fixed value of
A_{*}
and any fixed integer
N_{0}\geq 1
, we choose

N
to be sufficiently large (in a manner that depends on
N_{0},A_{*},\mathfrak{D},q,
and
\upsigma
)
and then
\varepsilon
to be sufficiently small
(in a manner that depends on
N,N_{0},A_{*},\mathfrak{D},q,
and
\upsigma
)
such that all of the previous estimates proved in the paper hold true.
For this fixed value of
A_{*}
, this justifies inequality (
5.57
).
We now note that the negativity
of the factor
C_{*}-A_{*}
ensures that we can discard the first time integral on RHS (
5.57
),
that is, for
t\in(T_{\textnormal{Boot}},1]
,
we have

\mathbb{D}^{2}(t)\leq C\mathring{\upepsilon}^{2}+C\int_{t}^{1}s^{-1+\upsigma}\mathbb{D}^{2}(s)\,ds
.
From this inequality and Grönwall’s lemma, we deduce that

\mathbb{D}^{2}(t)\leq C\mathring{\upepsilon}^{2}
.
From this estimate and (
5.6
),
we conclude the desired bound (
5.2
).

∎

5.10
Existence of perturbed solutions on the entire half-slab
(0,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{\mathfrak{D}}

In the next proposition, we use the a priori estimates of
Proposition
5.1
and standard local well-posedness/continuation results
to show that the perturbed solution exists on
(0,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{\mathfrak{D}}
.

Proposition 5.28

(Existence of perturbed solutions on the entire half-slab
{(0,1]}\times\mathbb{T}^{\mathfrak{D}}
)
.

Let
(\Sigma_{1}=\mathbb{T}^{\mathfrak{D}},\mathring{g},\mathring{k},\mathring{\psi},\mathring{\phi})

be geometric initial data (see Sect.
1.1
)
for the Einstein-scalar field equations verifying the constraint equations (
1.2a
)–(
1.2b
) and the CMC condition
\mathrm{tr}k=-1
(see Remark
1.5
),
and let
\{\mathring{e}_{I}\}_{I=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}

be the initial orthonormal frame (on
\Sigma_{1}
)
constructed in Sect.
5.11
.
Recall that

\mathbb{L}_{(n)}(t),\mathbb{H}_{(n)}(t)
,
and
\mathbb{D}(t)
are norms from Definition
3.1

and that
\mathring{\upepsilon}:=\mathbb{D}(1)
(see (
5.1
)).
Assume that the following conditions are satisfied:

•

N_{0}\geq 1
.

•

A_{*}\geq 1
is sufficiently large.

•

N
is sufficiently large in a manner
that depends on
N_{0},A_{*},\mathfrak{D},q,
and
\upsigma
.

•

The norm
\mathring{\upepsilon}
defined in (
5.1
)
is sufficiently small
in a manner that depends on
N,N_{0},A_{*},\mathfrak{D},q,
and
\upsigma
.

Then there exists a constant
C_{N,N_{0},A_{*},\mathfrak{D},q,\upsigma}>0

such these data launch a perturbed solution

(n,k_{IJ},\upgamma_{IJB},e_{I}^{i},\upomega_{i}^{I},\psi)_{I,J,B,i=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}

to the reduced equations of Proposition
2.2

that exists classically on
(0,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{\mathfrak{D}}

and satisfies the following estimate for
t\in(0,1]
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}(t)+\mathbb{L}_{(n)}(t)+\mathbb{H}_{(n)}(t)

\displaystyle\leq C_{N,N_{0},A_{*},\mathfrak{D},q,\upsigma}\mathring{\upepsilon}.

(5.58)

Moreover, if we define
g_{ij}
and
{\bf{g}}
in terms of the reduced variables by

g_{ij}:=\upomega_{i}^{A}\upomega_{j}^{A}

and

{\bf g}:=-n^{2}dt\otimes dt+g_{ab}dx^{a}\otimes dx^{b}

(where
t
is the CMC time function and

\{x^{i}\}_{i=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}
are the transported spatial coordinates),
then the tensorfields
({\bf g},\psi)

are also classical solutions to the Einstein-scalar field system (
1.1a
)–(
1.1b
)
on
(0,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{\mathfrak{D}}
.

Proof.

We first fix
N_{0}\geq 1
,
A_{*}
sufficiently large, and
N
sufficiently large
such that if the bootstrap smallness parameter
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small, then
all of the estimates proved in the previous subsections hold true
on
(T_{\textnormal{Boot}},1]\times\mathbb{T}^{\mathfrak{D}}
,
as long as the bootstrap assumption (
3.10
) holds for
t\in(T_{\textnormal{Boot}},1]
.
By standard local well-posedness, if
\mathring{\upepsilon}
is
sufficiently small and
C
is sufficiently large,
then there exists a minimal time

T_{\textnormal{Min}}\in[0,1)
, such that the solution

(n,k,\upgamma,e,\upomega,n,\psi)

to the reduced equations of Proposition
2.2

exists classically for
(t,x)\in(T_{\textnormal{Min}},1]\times\mathbb{T}^{\mathfrak{D}}
and such that the
bootstrap assumptions (
3.10
) hold with
T_{\textnormal{Boot}}=T_{\textnormal{Min}}
and
\varepsilon:=C\mathring{\upepsilon}
.
By enlarging
C
if necessary,
we can assume that
C\geq 2C_{N,N_{0},A_{*},\mathfrak{D},q,\upsigma}
, where
C_{N,N_{0},A_{*},\mathfrak{D},q,\upsigma}

is the constant on RHS (
5.2
). For the reader’s convenience, we now comment on
the “standard local well-posedness” mentioned above. Specifically, readers
can consult
[
6
]
for the main ideas behind the proof of local well-posedness
in a similar but distinct elliptic-hyperbolic gauge for Einstein’s equations,
or
[
59
, Theorem 14.1]
for a sketch of a proof
of local well-posedness in CMC-transported spatial coordinates;
local well-posedness for the equations of Proposition
2.2

can be proved via similar arguments.
We emphasize that, as is stated in Proposition
2.2
,
solutions to the reduced equations
(including the constraints)
are also solutions to the Einstein-scalar field system (
1.1a
)–(
1.1b
),
where the spacetime metric can be reconstructed from the reduced variables via the equations

{\bf g}=-n^{2}dt\otimes dt+g_{ab}dx^{a}\otimes dx^{b}

and

g_{ij}=g(\partial_{i},\partial_{j})=\upomega_{i}^{A}\upomega_{j}^{A}

(see (
2.7
) and (
2.3
)).
Moreover, in view of the norms defined in Definition
3.1
,
it is a standard result (again, see
[
6
]
for the main ideas)
that if
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small, then either
i)

T_{\textnormal{Min}}=0
or

ii)

T_{\textnormal{Min}}\in(0,1)
and
the bootstrap assumptions
are saturated on the time interval
(T_{\textnormal{Min}},1]
, that is,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in(T_{\textnormal{Min}},1]}\left\{\mathbb{D}(t)+\mathbb{L}_{(n)}(t)+\mathbb{H}_{(n)}(t)\right\}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon.

(5.59)

The latter possibility is ruled out by inequality (
5.2
) when
\mathring{\upepsilon}
is small enough.
Thus,
T_{\textnormal{Min}}=0
. In particular, the solution exists classically for
(t,x)\in(0,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{\mathfrak{D}}
,
and the estimate (
5.58
) holds for
t\in(0,1]
.
∎

5.11
Construction of the initial orthonormal spatial frame

Thus far, we have not explained how to construct
the initial orthonormal spatial frame
\{\mathring{e}_{I}\}_{I=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}

on
\Sigma_{1}
. To achieve this away from symmetry,
we simply apply the Gram–Schmidt process to the transported spatial coordinate vectorfield frame

\{\partial_{i}\}_{i=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}
.
More precisely, with
\mathring{g}
denoting the Riemannian metric on
\Sigma_{1}
,
we set:

\displaystyle\mathring{e}_{1}

\displaystyle:=\frac{\partial_{1}}{\sqrt{\mathring{g}_{11}}}=\frac{\partial_{1}}{\sqrt{\mathring{g}(\partial_{1},\partial_{1})}},

(5.60a)

\displaystyle\mathring{E}_{M+1}

\displaystyle:=\partial_{M+1}-\sum_{L=1,\cdots,M}\underbrace{\mathring{g}_{cd}\updelta_{M+1}^{c}\mathring{e}_{L}^{d}}_{\mathring{g}(\partial_{M+1},\mathring{e_{L}})}\mathring{e_{L}},

\displaystyle M=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}-1,

(5.60b)

\displaystyle\mathring{e}_{M+1}

\displaystyle:=\frac{\mathring{E}_{M+1}}{\sqrt{\mathring{g}_{cd}\mathring{E}_{M+1}^{c}\mathring{E}_{M+1}^{d}}},

\displaystyle M=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}-1.

(5.60c)

By construction, for
1\leq I,J\leq\mathfrak{D}
,
we have the desired identity

\mathring{g}(\mathring{e}_{I},\mathring{e}_{J})=\updelta_{IJ}
,
where
\updelta_{IJ}
is the Kronecker delta.

In the polarized
U(1)
-symmetric case with
\mathfrak{D}=3
,
we proceed in a similar fashion,
but starting with

\mathring{e}_{3}:=\frac{\partial_{3}}{\sqrt{\mathring{g}_{33}}}=\frac{\partial_{3}}{\sqrt{\mathring{g}(\partial_{3},\partial_{3})}}
.
Note that for metrics that are initially polarized and
U(1)
-symmetric in the
sense described in Lemma
2.3
,
this Gram–Schmidt process leads to an initial frame that respects the
\partial_{3}
symmetry:

\mathcal{L}_{\partial_{3}}\mathring{e}_{I}=0
for
I=1,2,3
.
Hence, Lemma
2.4
ensures that throughout the classical evolution,
we have

e_{3}=\frac{\partial_{3}}{\sqrt{g_{33}}}

and

\mathcal{L}_{\partial_{3}}e_{I}=0
for
I=1,2,3
.

5.12
The near-Kasner smallness condition on the geometric initial data

Before proving our main theorems, we will first define a norm of
the “geometric” initial data

(\Sigma_{1}=\mathbb{T}^{\mathfrak{D}},\mathring{g},\mathring{k},\mathring{\psi},\mathring{\phi})

minus the background Kasner data.
The smallness of this difference will be sufficient for the validity of our main results.
We highlight that the lapse
n
is not among the geometric initial data;
it is a gauge-dependent quantity that can be controlled in terms of the geometric data.
Then, in Lemma
5.29
,
we show that if the geometric data are sufficiently near-Kasner,
then the full data norm

\mathbb{D}(1)+\mathbb{L}_{(n)}(1)+\mathbb{H}_{(n)}(1)

is small, i.e., we have smallness not only for the geometric data,
but also for all of the gauge-dependent quantities such as
n-1
,

e_{I}^{i}-\widetilde{e}_{I}^{i}
,

k_{IJ}-\widetilde{k}_{IJ}
,
etc.

To proceed, we let
(\Sigma_{1}=\mathbb{T}^{\mathfrak{D}},\mathring{g},\mathring{k},\mathring{\psi},\mathring{\phi})

be a geometric initial data set, as described in Sect.
1.1
.
Recall that relative to standard coordinates on
\mathbb{T}^{\mathfrak{D}}
,
the Kasner background data (on
\Sigma_{1}
) have the following components:

\mathring{g}_{ij}^{\textnormal{KAS}}:=\updelta_{ij}
,

\mathring{k}_{ij}^{\textnormal{KAS}}:=-\widetilde{q}_{\underline{i}}\updelta_{\underline{i}j}
,

\mathring{\psi}^{\textnormal{KAS}}:=0
,

\mathring{\phi}^{\textnormal{KAS}}:=\widetilde{B}
,
where
\updelta_{ij}
is the Kronecker delta,
we do not sum over repeated underlined indices,
and by assumption, the Kasner exponent constraints (
1.7
) are satisfied.
For
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we define the following norm which,
relative to the standard coordinates on
\mathbb{T}^{\mathfrak{D}}
, measures the perturbation of the geometric initial data
set away from the Kasner background:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathring{\upalpha}=\mathring{\upalpha}(N):=&\sum_{i,j=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}\|\mathring{g}_{ij}-\updelta_{ij}\|_{H^{N+1}(\mathbb{T}^{\mathfrak{D}})}+\sum_{i,j=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}\|\mathring{k}_{ij}+\widetilde{q}_{\underline{i}}\updelta_{\underline{i}j}\|_{H^{N}(\mathbb{T}^{\mathfrak{D}})}\\
&\ \ +\|\mathring{\psi}\|_{H^{N+1}(\mathbb{T}^{\mathfrak{D}})}+\|\mathring{\phi}-\widetilde{B}\|_{H^{N}(\mathbb{T}^{\mathfrak{D}})}.\end{split}

(5.61)

In the next lemma, we show that the norms appearing in the bootstrap assumptions (
3.10
)
are initially small, provided
\mathring{\upalpha}
is sufficiently small.

Lemma 5.29

(A near-Kasner smallness condition on the geometric initial data implies smallness
of all reduced solution variables along
\Sigma_{1}
)
.

Recall that
\mathbb{D}(t)
is the total norm of the dynamic variables
and that
\mathbb{L}_{(n)}
and
\mathbb{H}_{(n)}
are the norms of the lapse
(see Definition
3.1
).
For
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we define:

\displaystyle\mathring{\upeta}=\mathring{\upeta}(N)

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{D}(1)+\mathbb{L}_{(n)}(1)+\mathbb{H}_{(n)}(1).

(5.62)

Let
\mathring{\upalpha}
be the norm of the perturbation of the geometric initial data away from the Kasner data,
as defined in (
5.61
). Let

\{\mathring{e}_{I}\}_{I=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}

be the initial orthonormal frame
constructed in Sect.
5.11
,
and let the initial lapse
\mathring{n}:=n|_{\Sigma_{1}}
be the solution
to the elliptic PDE (
2.25
) (with
t=1
).
Fix
N_{0}\geq 1
.
There exists a constant
C=C_{N,N_{0},\mathfrak{D}}>0

such that if
N
is sufficiently large in a manner that depends on
N_{0}
and
\mathfrak{D}
,
and if
\mathring{\upalpha}
is sufficiently small,
then:

\displaystyle\mathring{\upeta}

\displaystyle\leq C\mathring{\upalpha}.

(5.63)

In particular, since the initial norm
\mathring{\upepsilon}
of the dynamic variables
defined in (
5.1
) satisfies
\mathring{\upepsilon}\leq\mathring{\upeta}
,
it follows from (
5.63
) that:

\displaystyle\mathring{\upepsilon}

\displaystyle\leq C\mathring{\upalpha}.

(5.64)

Sketch of the proof.

This is a standard result, so we will only sketch the proof.
Throughout, we will assume that

\mathring{\upalpha}
is sufficiently small.
From (
5.61
), we see that
the
\mathfrak{D}\times\mathfrak{D}
matrix
\mathring{g}_{ij}

is equal to the identity matrix up to an error matrix whose components are bounded
in the norm
\|\cdot\|_{H^{N+1}(\mathbb{T}^{\mathfrak{D}})}

by
\lesssim\mathring{\upalpha}
.
From this fact,
the Gram–Schmidt process described in Sect.
5.11
,
and the standard Sobolev calculus (i.e., estimates of the type appearing in Lemma
4.1
),
it follows that for
1\leq I,i\leq\mathfrak{D}
,
we have

\|\mathring{e}_{I}^{i}-\updelta_{I}^{i}\|_{H^{N+1}(\mathbb{T}^{\mathfrak{D}})}\lesssim\mathring{\upalpha}
,
where
\updelta_{I}^{i}
denotes the Kronecker delta.
To complete the proof of (
5.63
),
we must show that when
t=1
,
the remaining norms in Definition
3.1
are all
\lesssim\mathring{\upalpha}
.
This can be achieved by working relative to the
standard spatial coordinates
\{x^{i}\}_{i=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}
on
\Sigma_{1}

and using the definition of
\mathring{\upalpha}
,
the definitions of the quantities appearing in the norms of Definition
3.1
,
the standard Sobolev calculus, and elliptic estimates for the lapse, similar to the ones we used to prove
Proposition
5.3
. As one example, we will show that

\|\upgamma_{IJB}\|_{H^{N}(\Sigma_{1})}\lesssim\mathring{\upalpha}
.
First, we note that

\upgamma_{IJB}|_{\Sigma_{1}}=\mathring{g}_{ab}\mathring{e}_{I}^{c}(\partial_{c}\mathring{e}_{J}^{a})\mathring{e}_{B}^{b}+\mathring{e}_{I}^{i}\mathring{e}_{J}^{j}\mathring{e}_{B}^{b}\mathring{\Gamma}_{ibj}
,
where
\mathring{\Gamma}_{ibj}=\frac{1}{2}\left\{\partial_{i}\mathring{g}_{bj}+\partial_{j}\mathring{g}_{ib}-\partial_{b}\mathring{g}_{ij}\right\}

are the (lowered) Christoffel symbols of
\mathring{g}
relative to the spatial coordinates

\{x^{i}\}_{i=1,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}}
on
\Sigma_{1}
.
Thus, from this expression for
\upgamma_{IJB}|_{\Sigma_{1}}
,
definition (
5.61
),
the estimates
\|\mathring{e}_{I}^{i}-\updelta_{I}^{i}\|_{H^{N+1}(\mathbb{T}^{\mathfrak{D}})}\lesssim\mathring{\upalpha}

and
\|\mathring{g}_{ij}-\updelta_{ij}\|_{H^{N+1}(\mathbb{T}^{\mathfrak{D}})}\lesssim\mathring{\upalpha}
,
and the standard Sobolev calculus,
we conclude the desired bound
\|\upgamma_{IJB}\|_{H^{N}(\Sigma_{1})}\leq C\mathring{\upalpha}
.
This concludes our proof sketch.
∎

6
The two stable blowup theorems

In this section, we prove our two main theorems.
The derivation of the a priori estimate (
5.58
)
was the difficult part of the proof, and based on this estimate,
the proofs of the main results will unfold in a natural fashion.

6.1
Statement of the theorems

In this section, we state the two theorems.
